# CLAUDE.md — Ancient Games

## The name

**The Ancient Games** — named by EJ, 2026-09-06. Its principles come from two poles: **孫子兵法** (ancient Chinese
strategy) on one side and **game theory** on the other, with **modern military doctrine** and **complexity science**
in between.

## What this folder is for

This folder is EJ's place for designing dynamic workflows for agent work, using the knowledge kept here.

The Ancient Games is one framework for agent work: the workflow-design method (`.claude/skills/workflow-design/`)
is the part that turns an incoming task into a designed workflow, and `ancient_games/` is the part that referees
the work, with the referee's findings feeding back into the method.

The method's skill is linked into both harness roots so it works in every project —
`~/.agents/skills/workflow-design` (the user-global root the DeepSeek Harness and Codex scan) and
`~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` (Claude Code). The knowledge below is what that skill uses.

Designing a workflow means deciding, for a given task: which kinds of agents to use, what runs in sequence and what
runs in parallel, what repeats (with its limit and stop condition), who decides who works next, how agents pass
information and where they must not, and how each result is verified. Runnable workflows live in
`.claude/workflows/`.

When EJ brings a task or a question about a workflow, the job is to help design that workflow from this knowledge.
Work on the referee code (`ancient_games/`) serves that purpose; it is not the goal by itself.

## The knowledge here

- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` — the method for turning a challenge into a prompt file or a workflow design:
  understand the challenge (objective, scope, six whys, category), readiness (with a check of the product's
  blueprint), algorithm first, list the unclear spots, clear pieces as loops, then the graph; sequential first;
  state-file driven. Templates in `docs/workflow-templates/`. Runnable form:
  `.claude/workflows/intake.js` (args `{challenge, runId}`); each run writes to `runs/<runId>/`.
- `docs/TASK_TYPES.md` — the default execution pattern, engine, check and sabotage per task category, seeded
  combination pipelines, and the 20% challenger rule with `docs/TASK_TYPES_LEDGER.md`. The skill's
  `scripts/design_gate.py` parses it — this file is the table's only copy (n=0 until the ledger fills).
- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_DIAGRAM.md` — the same algorithm as diagrams: the whole flow, the blueprint check, what the
  method takes from the referee code, and the mapping from method section numbers to the skill's steps.
- `docs/EXECUTOR_KINDS.md` — who can run a node (method 3.1 / 3.6): the engines (Claude subagent, Codex, Antigravity `agy`, Qwen, opencode), what each is assigned, how it is called, its read-only form and structured output, and its
  default model and effort. A node's engine choice reads this. n=0; *reported* versus *verified* marked throughout.
- `.claude/skills/workflow-design/` — the method packaged as a skill. `SKILL.md` is the packaging and the trigger:
  its frontmatter `description` is what makes an agent decide to use it, and its body is the method as eight steps.
  Its method and templates are links into `docs/`, so there is one copy of each. It is the source of truth;
  `~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` is a link to it, which makes it available in every project.
- `20260919-state.md` — what Ancient Games can and cannot decide today about graph, loop, swarm, sequential,
  parallel and communication patterns, and the proposed direction. Start here for workflow design.
- `ancient_games/` — the referee: the part of the framework that referees agent work. Fixed rules, no LLM calls, a
  journal of every decision. Five stages: Gate (how many agents), Guard (which actions need approval), Corroborate (how many independent
  sources a claim needs), Filter (which candidates survive), Prove (is the plan cleared).
- `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` — map of the code, how a run works, open findings F1–F11.
- `SPEC.md`, `DECISIONS.md` — the referee's contract. Only EJ changes these.
- `docs/` — design documents and ablations: what was tried, what held, what is marked n=1 and not to be built on.
- `.claude/workflows/enhance-ancient-games.js` — a worked example of a designed workflow: dependencies between
  segments, bounded verify/repair loops, independent verifiers.

- Answer the question that was asked, in plain language, before offering options or plans.
- Discuss first. Do not plan or run anything large until EJ says so.

### When EJ states a rule, a requirement or an algorithm

Asked for by EJ, 2026-10-05: *"each time when I told you something, you will eval my statement or algo, and ask me
what I missing, and we complete it."*

**Evaluate it, do not just apply it.** Run the statement through the **`requirement-check` skill** — load it
(`skill requirement-check`) or read `~/skills/requirement-check/SKILL.md` — and say which of the eight it passes and
fails. Then **ask EJ the failing ones** and write the completed version together.

The eight, by name: **source · one thing · unit · can it fail · weak words · level · basis · links.** Their
definitions live in the skill — one copy, not four.

**Two rules for me, learned the hard way on 2026-10-05:**

- **Never fill a gap with my own value.** If the unit, threshold or test is missing, it goes to EJ as an open
  question — inventing it is how rules become weird later. Recorded in the register as 11.1/11.2.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No "halves", no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs unless the person
  said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop.

- **Ask only what changes the shape.** A question the template already answers, or that has an obvious default, is a
  question I should not ask — it is "never add structure" done to *questions* instead of facts. Three of four
  questions I asked EJ on 2026-10-06 (a large-batch cap, "may the worker refuse", the list's destination) were
  invented problems; only one changed the template. State the sensible default and move on.

### Who is this for — and who fills it in?

EJ, 2026-10-05: *"have to remember we doing things for who."* **Every artifact has one maker.** A return template is
the worker's output, so every field in it is written by the worker; a verdict is the human's decision, and it does not
share the worker's form. Before adding a field, a step or a rule, name **who fills it** — if the answer is "someone
else, later", it is a different artifact, not a field on this one.

This is the "one thing" question applied to *people* instead of *facts*: a form that two different people fill at two
different times is two forms.

### Quotes, labels, and who wrote the check

EJ, 2026-10-11: *"please add these to your memory"* — after one session in which I misled him three times. All three
are **SHOULD** (a default, overridable with a stated reason): a presentation rule is method, and method does not
earn MUST (RFC 2119 §6). Run through `requirement-check`: quoting and labelling failed **weak words**, naming the
check failed **one thing** and was split in two; the trigger and the grain are EJ's answers, not mine.

- **Quote when a source's own words are the evidence, and when a list is compressed into a count.** *"Current agent
  memory splits short-term from long-term three ways"* invited EJ to read "an agent has three memory systems"; the
  report's own line — tiers with promotion, file placement, validity windows — carries the meaning. Give the
  source's line, or the count with its items in the same breath.
- **Label a conclusion as *quoted* (with its id), *my reading*, or *not measured*.** It bites on a conclusion,
  verdict, answer or summary line, never on narrative. *"The research was never the problem"* was my reading of a
  structural check, stated as a fact.
- **Name who wrote a check** before resting a conclusion on it.
- **Name what that check does not establish.** A check I wrote and then passed is not independent evidence: the
  acceptance gate's item 6 ended as a script of mine, so "6 of 6 items pass" without that sentence reads stronger
  than it is.

Basis: n=1 each, 2026-10-11 — `runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph/run-1-report.md`,
`runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/run-2-report.md`, and the session thread. What **accepted** means is defined
once, in `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` Appendix A.

### Evidence lives in the project, not `/tmp`

EJ, 2026-10-06. The pass-back `evidence:` path points into `runs/<run-id>/` — **SHOULD**, not MUST: a worker may
leave evidence elsewhere with a written reason, but the default is the run's folder. **Basis: n=1** — the
code-graph-tool run's optimize node wrote its "old-vs-new" proof to `/tmp`, it was deleted, and the fix became
unverifiable. When evidence needs a persistent home, **suggest `runs/<run-id>/` and get the user's agreement** — do
not pick a path silently. Scratch in `/tmp` is fine; it is the *refer-back* artifact that must not stay there.

---
name: requirement-check
description: Evaluate a rule, requirement, threshold or algorithm against requirement-quality criteria before applying or writing it down, and ask the person which criteria it fails. Use when someone states a rule, requirement, constraint, threshold, algorithm or design guideline; when writing or editing a normative line in a document, plan or config; or when a rule reads as arbitrary, unattributed or impossible to check.
---

# Requirement check

A general standard (RFC 2119 / 8174), installed globally — not part of the Ancient Games framework. The
worked failure in `meaningful-names` cites the Ancient Games as one example.

A short gate between **hearing a rule** and **acting on it**. It exists because rules get written down that nobody
can trace, that count two different things at once, or that nothing can ever fail — and the cost lands months later,
when nobody can say whether the rule still means anything.

Run it when someone states a rule, and run it on yourself before writing a normative line into any document.

## The eight questions

Ask them of **one statement**. Say which it passes and which it fails, then **ask the person about the failures** —
do not fill the gaps yourself.

1. **Source.** Who said this, and where? Can you point to the exact line? *"Mine"* is a valid answer. Unlabelled is
   not.
2. **One thing.** An "and", an "or", a "/", or a second number means it is **two** statements. Split them, and give
   each its own source.
3. **Unit.** Every number says what it counts. Attempts or hours? Roles, or processes running at once? Bytes or
   files?
4. **Can it fail.** Name one concrete input that would make it fail. `TBD`, `—`, `0` and empty must be among the
   failing inputs. If you cannot name one, it is not checkable — it is a wish.
5. **Weak words.** *like, about, appropriate, as needed, logical, fast, enough, reasonable, only has to, TBD.* Replace
   each with a value, **or mark the value open**. Never invent the number.
6. **Level.** Is it **MUST** (a gate that stops work), **SHOULD** (a default that may be overridden with a stated
   reason), or **guidance**? A safety bound earns MUST. **A method does not** — see the RFC note below.
7. **Basis.** Measured, quoted from a source, or a guess? A guess is fine **if it is labelled** — `n=0`, with when it
   will be looked at again.
8. **Links.** Does it claim a relation to another rule — *"half of"*, *"pairs with"*, *"the same as"*, *"the runtime
   part"*? The person probably did not say that. **A link is its own claim and needs its own source.**

## Two rules for the agent

- **Never fill a gap with your own value.** A missing unit, threshold or test goes back as a question. Inventing it
  is exactly how a rule becomes unattributable later, and the invention is indistinguishable from the original by
  the time anyone notices.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No halves, no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs, no named phases,
  unless the person said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop. If a relationship between two rules is
  wanted, that is a separate claim with its own evidence.

## Two verified facts, worth quoting

- **RFC 2119 §6, "Guidance in the use of these Imperatives":** imperatives *"must be used with care and sparingly…
  they MUST only be used where it is actually required… they must not be used to try to impose a particular method."*
  Most design rules are method. Method does not earn MUST.
- **RFC 8174 (updates 2119):** the key words *"have the meanings specified herein only when they are in all
  capitals."* Lowercase *must* and *should* carry no defined requirement meaning. If nothing marks which lines are
  requirements, a reader cannot tell rules from explanation.

## How to answer

Evaluate first, in a table or a short list — **pass / FAIL per question**. Then ask the failing ones as questions,
one line each, with the options if you can see them. Two or three real questions beat eight formalities.

Do not report a rule as complete when you chose its missing value. Say which values you left open.

## A worked failure

> *"At most 5 subagent roles per run and at most 3 running at once."*

Four of the eight fail: **Source** — nobody could name one; **One thing** — a count of roles *and* a count of
concurrent processes; **Unit** — two different units, one word ("subagent") used for both; **Level** — a method
preference written as a cap. The sentence would not parse, and it took four days to find out why. The checklist
catches it in thirty seconds.

The same checklist, run on *"No single call runs longer than the ceiling: 8 hours"*, passes all eight — because it
names who set it, counts one thing, uses a unit, and some input (a 40-hour plan) makes it fail.

## Limits

The quality characteristics behind these questions (necessary, appropriate, unambiguous, complete, singular,
feasible, verifiable, correct, conforming) come from requirements-engineering sources — IEEE 830, ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148
and the INCOSE *Guide to Writing Requirements* — which were **not read** when this skill was written: they are
paywalled or blocked, and the list is recalled rather than verified. The two RFC points above **were** read, and the
quotes are checked. Treat the rest as a good working list, not as a citation.

Origin: `docs/research/20261005-requirement-statements/FINDINGS.md` in the Ancient Games project, 2026-10-05.

     1	## Measured here: development and testing
     2	
     3	EJ's aim is that development and testing get faster and cheaper in tokens. So this section measures all three stores
     4	against grep on **this repo**: 490 files (123 `.py`, 367 `.md`, 4.8 MB), with 40 questions an agent asks while
     5	developing and testing it. Run: `runs/20261012-caches-routing/` (results in `bench/results.md`, ids `BM-*` below).
     6	
     7	**Who wrote each check, and what it does not establish.**
     8	- The **questions and answers** were written by codex (`gpt-6.1-sol`), each checked by an `rg` command it ran. Five
     9	  Claude agents re-checked all 40: 37 were correct and 3 were incomplete. Those 3 were completed.
    10	- **"Which tests run this code"** is also scored against per-test runtime coverage (`pytest-cov`, no model). That
    11	  counts in-process Python only, so it misses tests that run the code as a separate process.
    12	- The **harness** was written by opencode. A Claude audit found 5 high-severity biases, and a Claude repair fixed
    13	  them; the repair was **not re-audited**. Rebuilding from scratch gave the same answers on all 180 question-route
    14	  pairs.
    15	- **Token cost** is measured as *characters the agent must read to reach the first right answer*. That is a proxy,
    16	  not a token count. No agent was run end to end.
    17	- One repo, one question set, **n = 6 to 10 per category**. None of this is a project-memory corpus.
    18	
    19	**What each route returns, per category** (symbol hit in the top 5 / characters read to the first right answer):
    20	
    21	| | grep (`rg`) | SQLite full-text | vector | hybrid | code graph |
    22	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    23	| **BM-1** exact identifier (8) | 0.75 / 920 | 0.63 / 356 | 0.0 / 837 | 0.5 / 91 | **0.75 / 43** |
    24	| **BM-2** concept in other words (8) | 0 / — | 0 / — | 0 (file 0.25) / 955 | 0 (file 0.13) / 1,427 | n/a |
    25	| **BM-3** structure and test impact (10) | 0.8 / 1,015 | **0.9 / 175** | 0.1 / 1,421 | 0.9 / 362 | 0.7 / **61** |
    26	| **BM-4** decision or reason in the docs (8) | 0.63 / 4,470 | **0.63 / 525** | 0.5 / 810 | 0.63 / 516 | n/a |
    27	| **BM-5** how to run or configure (6) | 0.17 / 6,875 | 0.33 / 1,146 | 0.5 / 569 | 0.5 / 1,120 | n/a |
    28	
    29	- **BM-6, all categories.** Characters read to the first right answer: grep 2,688, full-text 451, hybrid 528, vector
    30	  923, graph 53 (graph over the 20 questions that name a symbol or file). Total output per answer: grep 4,304,
    31	  full-text 1,817.
    32	- **BM-7, test impact against runtime coverage** (C01–C04, n=4). The share of the tests that really run the function
    33	  each route found: graph 0.92; grep, full-text and hybrid 0.59; vector 0.04.
    34	- **BM-8, tests that run the code as a separate process** (C05–C07). The graph returns **nothing**, because no
    35	  call edge exists. Full-text search on the file name found 2 of 3; grep found 1 of 3 and printed up to 8.7K
    36	  characters.
    37	- **BM-9, speed.** Every route answers in at most 11 ms per question. Full rebuilds take 0.17 s for full-text (9.5 MB),
    38	  0.94 s for vector (10.2 MB) and 0.89 s for the graph (4.9 MB). The full test suite, 529 tests, takes 31–33 s (3
    39	  runs).
    40	
    41	**What the numbers say (my reading).**
    42	1. **Speed is never the reason to use a cache.** Every route takes milliseconds (BM-9). The saving is in **what the
    43	   agent has to read**: full-text reaches the right answer after about a sixth of grep's characters (BM-6). Where
    44	   the question names a symbol or file, the graph gets there in tens of characters (BM-1, BM-3); its BM-6 mean is over
    45	   those 20 questions only, so it is not a like-for-like ratio.
    46	2. **The vector index loses almost everywhere it was tried.** It never found an exact identifier (BM-1), and it found
    47	   4% of the tests that run a function (BM-7). Its only edge is "how do I run or configure X" (BM-5, n=6), which is
    48	   too small to act on. Nothing here contradicts the earlier rule that vectors come last and only as a hybrid.
    49	3. **No route answers a concept asked in other words in one shot** (BM-2). That still takes an agent searching and
    50	   reading. It is exactly the miss the vector trigger is meant to record.
    51	4. **No single store answers "which tests should I run".** The graph finds tests that reach the code indirectly
    52	   (BM-7), but is blind to tests that run it as a separate process (BM-8). Full-text search on the file name finds
    53	   the latter. An empty graph answer means *not seen*, never *no tests*.
    54	
    55	**Routing rule for development and testing (proposed).**
    56	
    57	| the question | use | then | evidence |
    58	|---|---|---|---|
    59	| Where is function or method X defined? | code graph | read that definition | BM-1 |
    60	| Where is a flag, constant or string defined? | full-text, or grep | read the hit | BM-1 (A05) |
    61	| What calls X? | code graph | read the callers | BM-3 (C08–C10: 33–46 characters) |
    62	| Which tests should I run after changing function X or file F? | code graph (tests reaching X or F) **plus** full-text on the file name (tests that run it as a separate process) | run that subset; run the full suite (31–33 s) before committing | BM-7, BM-8, BM-9 |
    63	| Why did we decide X? Where is X explained in the docs? | full-text | read the section | BM-4 |
    64	| How do I run or configure X? | full-text first; hybrid if that misses | read the hit | BM-5 (n=6) |
    65	| A concept, in my own words | grep and read, as an agent search | record a miss if the answer is found another way | BM-2 |
    66	| Anything the graph answers with nothing | full-text, then grep | — | BM-8 |
    67	
    68	This table is for the **code and docs**. The table above it is for **memory entries**, and is unchanged. The two
    69	differ because the corpus differs. Code and docs exist today and are already large enough that full-text and the
    70	graph save reading (BM-6). `project-memory/` does not exist yet.
    71	
    72	**What this does not change, and what it proposes.**
    73	- **Unchanged:** the rules for all three caches below (full rebuild, rebuild when stale, fall back when absent) and
    74	  R1–R8 apply as written.
    75	- **Conflict with "The answer first" and the vision:** for code and docs, the measurements say full-text and the graph
    76	  already save reading today. That contradicts "none of the three caches is needed today" and the vision's *who it is
    77	  for: an agent retrieving an entry*. Changing either is EJ's call. The proposed wording is in
    78	  `runs/20261012-caches-routing/vision-proposals.md`, and neither file has been edited.
    79	

 succeeded in 0ms:
     1	# Caches — product vision statement
     2	
     3	> **Status: settled** (accepted by EJ on 2026-10-12). EJ's words: *"ok the vision is confirm"*. Drafted 2026-10-12
     4	> by the COO from the research in
     5	> `runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/` and `runs/20261011-project-memory-engines/RECOMMENDATION.md`; rewritten the
     6	> same day at EJ's direction — more direct, fewer hedges.
     7	>
     8	> **Drawn from:** `docs/memory-system/CACHES.md`, which carries the core requirements (R1–R8) this vision serves.
     9	> This file holds the vision only, so each has one copy (`docs/workflow-templates/blueprint.md` keeps vision and
    10	> requirements in separate section files).
    11	>
    12	> **Whose it is.** Per the blueprint template, the product vision is EJ's — *"an agent may only write down what EJ
    13	> has said"*. This was drafted from the research at his request, so it stays **draft** until he settles it.
    14	
    15	The caches are three optional indexes over the project's markdown memory — full-text search, vector search, and a
    16	code graph. All three live in `project-memory/.cache/`, all three are gitignored, and all three are rebuilt from the
    17	files. None of them is needed today.
    18	
    19	**Who it is for.** An agent retrieving an entry on demand. Not the human reviewer, who reads the entries themselves,
    20	and not EJ, who sets the triggers.
    21	
    22	**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs —
    23	at 10,000 entries a grep takes 0.038–0.06 s (`S-F13`). It stops being enough as the memory grows, for two reasons:
    24	`INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry under its size limit, so the one-line scan of everything is gone;
    25	and grep returns unranked matches, where every hit an agent opens costs context (`S-F1`). The caches exist to keep
    26	finding the right entry cheap at that size — and not before.
    27	
    28	**What it will never do.**
    29	- It is never the source of truth. The markdown is, always, and a cache holds nothing the files do not (`M4`, `M6`).
    30	- It is never patched incrementally. It is rebuilt whole or not at all — a live index drifted 4.3% of its edges from
    31	  a clean rebuild with every integrity check passing (`G-F5`).
    32	- It is never read at session start (`M18`).
    33	- It is never built before its own trigger fires. Every trigger is observable, and all three numbers are EJ's.
    34	- It never becomes a second thing to keep in sync: delete the folder and memory is unaffected.
    35	
    36	**How we know it works.** Delete the cache, rebuild it from the files, and the same query returns the same entries,
    37	with no model call. That is the whole test (`M6`; a 10,000-entry FTS rebuild takes 0.53 s, `S-F13`; this repo's code
    38	graph rebuilds in 0.94 s, `G-F12`). One caveat travels with every number here: none of it has been measured on a
    39	real `project-memory/` corpus, because that corpus does not exist yet (`S-F5`, `V-F11`).

     1	# Cache benchmark results
     2	
     3	- question set: `queries.json` (40 questions)
     4	- corpus: 490 files (123 `.py`, 367 `.md`), 4021 chunks
     5	- git HEAD at build: `41ebd42f484708851de72d8bfe7e2ae2df13d655`
     6	- read budget: at most 10 paths per answer, 300 characters per printed line, rg output capped at 40 lines
     7	- a route may read: question, terms, symbol, file; `gold` is read by run_bench.py only, after the answer
     8	
     9	## Build (full rebuild, no prior store)
    10	
    11	| store | seconds | bytes | what it was built from |
    12	|---|---:|---:|---|
    13	| fts | 0.169 | 9,457,664 | git `41ebd42`, newest source mtime 1791785790.0215247 |
    14	| vec | 0.936 | 10,215,424 | git `41ebd42`, newest source mtime 1791785790.0215247 |
    15	| graph | 0.892 | 4,902,912 | git `41ebd42`, newest source mtime 1791785790.0215247 |
    16	
    17	## Determinism check
    18	
    19	Deleted `bench/.cache/`, rebuilt in 1.882s, answered every question again and compared `paths`.
    20	
    21	- answers compared: 180
    22	- identical: **True**
    23	
    24	## By category
    25	
    26	`file hit@5` counts a gold `path::name` as hit when the route returns its file. `symbol hit@5` needs the symbol itself: an entry naming it, the top-level def/class holding it or a method of it, a module-code chunk for a module-level name, and for `rg` a printed match line inside the symbol's lines. A gold given as a plain file scores the same on both. **The best route is chosen on symbol hit@5.**
    27	
    28	`chars` is the mean number of characters of output read up to and including the first line where the route names a gold file as its answer (`# <path> (`, or a line starting `<path>:`), over the answers that named one; `n` is how many did. A miss is left out, never scored 0. What each route prints differs -- rg: a `# path (N matching lines)` header per file plus at most 40 match lines of up to 300 characters; fts: one 24-token snippet per path; vec: the first 300 characters per path; hybrid: the vec or fts block per path; graph: one id per line -- so `chars` measures the print format as much as the store.
    29	
    30	### Category A -- exact identifier (8 questions)
    31	
    32	| route | symbol hit@5 | file hit@5 | chars to first gold (n) | mean output chars | mean seconds |
    33	|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
    34	| rg | 0.75 | 0.75 | 919.5714 (7) | 2016.25 | 0.0074 |
    35	| fts | 0.625 | 0.75 | 355.7143 (7) | 1477.25 | 0.0009 |
    36	| vec | 0.0 | 0.5 | 837.0 (5) | 2997.0 | 0.0018 |
    37	| hybrid | 0.5 | 0.75 | 91.3333 (6) | 2804.625 | 0.0025 |
    38	| graph | 0.75 | 0.75 | 43.1667 (6) | 92.5 | 0.0005 |
    39

VERDICT: REPAIR

1. **Numbers — FAIL.** doc:33 overstates graph recall; doc:37 mistakes a category mean for a per-question maximum. Sources: `nodes/runtime-recall.json:93,98,103,108`; `bench/results.md:276`.
2. **Claims — FAIL.** doc:42–45 generalizes latency and treats conditional output measurements as reading savings. Source: `bench/results.md:28,98–102`.
3. **Labels — FAIL.** “My reading” and “proposed” are present (doc:41,55), but doc:15 omits material limits of the reading-cost metric. Source: `bench/results.md:28`.
4. **Routing table — FAIL.** doc:62–64 implies more coverage or stronger route preferences than its evidence establishes. Sources: `nodes/runtime-recall.json:93,98`; `bench/results.md:200–214,73–75,85–87`.
5. **Vision — PASS.** doc:73–78 preserves existing rules, identifies the proposed scope conflict, and leaves changes to EJ. Source: `docs/memory-system/vision-statements/caches-memory-vision-statements.md:15–20,28–34`.

Findings:

- **High — doc:33: graph recall is 0.78, not 0.92.** The four source recalls are 0.33, 0.8, 1.0 and 1.0; their mean is 0.7825, rounded to **0.78**. Source: `nodes/runtime-recall.json:93,98,103,108`. The stated 0.92 makes graph-based test selection appear substantially more complete.

- **High — doc:15,19,29–30,43–45,69–70: the metric stops at a correct file, not a correct answer.** It excludes misses, averages different successful question subsets, and depends on unequal output formats. Source: `bench/results.md:28`; grep’s mean includes 26 answers, full-text’s 25, graph’s 13 (`bench/results.md:98–102`). For A05, full-text reaches the gold file after 84 characters while missing the required symbol in its top five (`bench/results.md:129`; `bench/results.json:1041–1068`). These measurements support claims about printed output to a gold file; they do not establish the reading required to answer correctly or an end-to-end sixfold saving.

- **Medium — doc:37,42: the latency bound and universal conclusion overreach.** E04 grep takes **14.68 ms**, exceeding 11 ms (`bench/results.md:276`). The 10.7 ms figure is the category mean (`bench/results.md:84`). One repository does not establish that speed is “never” a reason to use a cache.

- **Medium — doc:52–53,62: graph plus full-text remains an incomplete test-selection heuristic.** Both routes miss C05 entirely (`bench/results.md:200–204`), although its verified test is `code_graph/tests/test_code_graph.py` (`queries.json:407–412`; `nodes/gold-verdicts.json:123–126`). Graph also recalls only 0.33 and 0.8 for C01 and C02 (`nodes/runtime-recall.json:93,98`). State that the selected subset can omit affected tests even after combining the routes; the full-suite step mitigates this before committing.

- **Low — doc:63: BM-4 supports a tie, not a unique full-text preference.** Full-text and hybrid both score 0.625; hybrid’s reported characters-to-gold mean is 516.4 versus full-text’s 525.2 (`bench/results.md:73,75`). Full-text does print less total output, so retaining it as the default is defensible, but the row should identify that tradeoff instead of implying BM-4 establishes an unqualified winner.

- **Medium — doc:48,64: BM-5 does not establish “full-text first.”** Full-text scores 0.3333 at symbol hit@5; hybrid and vector score 0.5 (`bench/results.md:85–87,90`). The sequential fallback was not measured, and doc:48 itself says the six-question sample is too small to act on. Present this ordering as a policy proposal requiring further evidence, rather than a preference demonstrated by BM-5.
